- M-18's full-channel body memory arrives simultaneously from the air: ground sensory memory is unified by the visual frame + Edmund's reverse river stone smile (bond peak)
- The way the door opens is a natural extension, not a teaching method.

**Ages 12-13: Understanding of systemic relationships + Perception of bodily decline + Loss of temperature**
- M-20 "Relationship between things"
- M-19 Two Hands
- M-21 temperature disappearance
- Narrowing is a drift, not an event.

**★ Penetration Constraint:**
- Edmund axis = reading dead objects (stone, water flow, machinery, pipelines, acoustics). He waited for her to get there before adding it.
- Miriam axis = Reading living things (birds, people, social relationships). Casual/Brief/No Explanation
- Two axes converge in Anna's body
- There's no scene where the core idea is "Anna fixed something." She **noticed** something.
- No prodigy displays. Perception is presented through bodily reactions (goosebumps, holding one's breath, goosebumps), not through intellectual conclusions.
Edmund was not a poet. He didn't give long speeches.

---

## VI. Edmund's prose Existence Density (Part 1)

**Part I-II:** Edmund is in almost every episode, but he doesn't do anything every time—sometimes he's just doing things in the background, and Anna smells the engine oil as she walks by. His presence is **environmental**—like a wall, like the sky, like the bells of an eagle's nest. Anna doesn't pay much attention to him.

**Part III:** Edmund is central to the flight episodes (flying with Anna, going to the outpost), but fades into the background in the everyday episodes. His presence shifts from "environment" to "companion." The two begin to discuss things—on an equal footing. The weight of the M-18 originates from this.

**Part IV:** Edmund is declining. His presence changes from "a person in space" to "a voice" (M-20 voice-only) to "silence" (M-21). Each stage of this decline goes unnoticed by Anna—drifting.

**Part V:** Edmund is gone, but his ghost echo is everywhere—when Anna teaches Milo, in her three-second pauses, in her habit of standing still and staring at one spot—**just unspoken.** One day, Miriam overheard Anna say something like, "Your dad said the same thing." Anna paused for half a second.

---

## VII. The cumulative position of the red jacket

See the Red Jacket Rules in Timelapse §P-06 to P-10.

**Key points accumulated in the childhood chapter:**
1. Part III, around age 10: Edmund gave her an old coat dyed with red clay. The sleeves were too long. "They'll fit perfectly when they're a little longer." (So casual that it's imperceptible on the first read)
2. Mid-Part III-IV: At a market/on a road in a valley, an old man's eyes light up slightly when he sees red (the first hint from Edmund's website, which Anna didn't notice).
3. Part IV: Before and after Edmund's disappearance, the red jacket hangs at the workshop entrance, and Anna passes by it every day.
4. Part IV finale: One cold morning, Anna put on her coat. A spring breeze. (Weight comes from all previous accumulations, not an emphasis)
5. Part V: The red jacket became Anna's visual signature. Anna was wearing this jacket when Joren said "You won't leave."
6. In Part V, Anna once said, "Just go to a place and make that place feel like it's the right person to come." (Quoting Edmund, she didn't realize she was quoting him.)

**Six times in total, they completed all the work for igniting Episode 61.**

---

## VIII. Relationship with Main Story Episodes 22-24

The sub-novel is the complete story. Episodes 22-24 are compressed fragments of memory from the main storyline. They function independently.

**Readers who have read the sub-novel carry double the weight in every flashback in Ep22-24.** Readers who haven't read it only get fragments, but the fragments themselves are powerful enough.

The portal structure in Ep22 uses a three-part "missing beat" to open the memory → return to Episode 1. All the M-module fragments triggered by Ep22-24 have a complete version in the sub-novel - the sub-novel gives these fragments depth.

The two systems operate independently but reinforce each other.

---

## IX. Finally: Reference for the texture and feel of a premium product

The texture of Sub-novel's prose should be referenced from:
- The quiet daily accumulation of Violet Evergarden
Nausicaä of the Valley of the Wind: A Physical World Experience
- The everyday wonder of Hayao Miyazaki's "Kiki's Delivery Service" and "Ponyo on the Cliff by the Sea"
- The craftsmanship, focus, and gentleness of "The Wind Rises"
- Texture density of the world in Spirited Away

**Not for reference:**
- The plot-driven pace of young adult adventure novels
- Internal monologue density of YA novels
- Any framework for the "development of geniuses"

Each episode should make the reader feel "I've lived a day in Archeon"—not "I've finished reading a chapter of the plot."


M-18b · Sky Narrows (12 years old · Late summer to early autumn)
Deployment location: The first half of Ep24. Between M-18 (bond peak / world's largest) and M-19 (world's first closing phase). Ensure Ep24's direction of motion is downward—from sky to ground.
Function: The transition of the world closing. Flight from daily → occasional → last time → gone. Perceived space from "spreading out in all directions" back to "bounced between two cliffs".
Rule: Narrowing is not an event—it's a drift. Anna didn't realize the sky was closing, just as she didn't realize it was opening. Edmund's decay is not the foreground of this module—the foreground is Anna's world shrinking in a physical sense. Edmund's decay is the cause, but the cause lies in the background layer.
The cumulative effect of the red jacket: Up to M-18b, the red jacket has appeared at least six times in M-15 (disembarking from the airship), M-16 (three fragments: sleeve/a sentence/the old man recognizes him), M-17 (waiting at the door), and M-18 (side of the ship)—all in the background, without any emphasis. The reader has by this point formed an unconscious visual baseline for "wearing red out." The weight of M-23 (wearing the red jacket) doesn't need dramaticizing—six background cumulations automatically make the moment of "wearing a jacket on a cold morning" appear heavier than it actually is. The red jacket doesn't appear once in this module—its absence itself is a signal.
Target: ~1,000 words.

Change doesn't begin on a single day.
That summer, Edmund went out less often. Before, he'd make a trip every few weeks—something in a settlement needed his attention, or there was a maintenance window at a transit station along a certain route. He'd take his toolbox on the airship, sometimes with Anna, sometimes not. When he returned, he smelled different; Anna could tell which way he'd gone just from the doorway.
That summer he missed two trips. The first was a planned trip—Anna saw him packing his toolbox the night before, and it was still at the door the next morning; Edmund said it was rescheduled. The second was a closer place, only half a day away. He told Joren to go instead.
Anna didn't ask why.
Flying began to recede from Anna's life around that time. It didn't suddenly stop one day—the frequency changed. Before, she could go on the airship almost every week, even if it was just a short maintenance trip with Edmund. Now it was every two weeks. Every three weeks. When was the last time she sat on the side of the ship and looked down? She wasn't sure. Not because of bad memory—it was because when something goes from "weekly" to "occasional," every step in between is so low that you don't even feel like you're going downstairs when you step on it.
During this time, Anna's world did something she didn't even notice: it retracted.
The world seen from three thousand meters above—a world unfolded like a map, where every shade of green seemed to speak, where underground water pipes "leaked" themselves through the colors of the vegetation—it hadn't disappeared. It was still there. But Anna was no longer at that height. She was in the Eagle's Nest. In the valley. On that fifty-meter walkway between Edmund's workshop and home.
The sound was the first to change.
Anna's ears grew accustomed to something special in the sky: vastness. At three thousand meters, sound scatters in all directions, nothing bounces it back. The wind doesn't bounce back and forth between two walls—it passes right by you and keeps going and going until you can't hear anything anymore. Anna experienced that shock when her ears first flew out of the canyon—the cliffs on both sides suddenly disappeared, the echo vanished, and her ears suddenly felt "empty." That emptiness later became a habit. Every sound she heard in the sky was one-way—it never looked back.
Now her ears are filled with echoes again.
Eagle's Nest Workshop sits atop a short, vertical cliff face on the upper part of the slope—sound scatters from the opposite slope, not simply bouncing back and forth, but spreading and layering along the gentle slope, becoming a warm reverberation. The hammering sounds from the valley floor, the winch sounds of the elevator, the low-frequency breathing of the windmills—they all rise up the slope, becoming weaker and more indistinct with each level, until at Eagle's Nest's height they become a unified texture where individual sound sources are indistinguishable—"the valley floor is at work." Anna's ears remember that this phenomenon doesn't exist in the sky—sounds only speak once, and then they're gone.
She didn't realize that this difference itself was a signal. She just felt that things she was hearing lately were getting noisier.

Last time
Edmund said one day that he wanted to go up and take a look.
It was a good day. He had spent the entire previous day in bed, and Miriam had brought him water three times. But this morning he sat up, dressed himself, and went to the door. The morning mist in Atheria had not yet completely dissipated—the sun was just peeking over the eastern cliffs, its light a pale yellow, like the slanting rays of late winter or early spring.
"Let's go?" he said.
Anna paused for half a second before realizing what he was saying.
The boat Joren had borrowed. Not the No. 3 that Edmund had borrowed before—that one had been sent to the northern route. Joren's boat was an older, slower, smaller short-haul vessel. A piece of wood on the deck was warped. As Edmund walked onto the deck, he grabbed the railing—the position where he had spent his entire life finding his balance on deck—but this time he gripped it for half a second longer than before.
Takeoff. Anna looked at him. He looked out the window.
They flew a circle over Atheria. Not far. He wasn't going anywhere—he was just looking. As they crossed the cliff top line, that acoustic openness returned to Anna's ears—she hadn't heard it in months—and she was surprised that she had almost forgotten what that openness felt like.
Edmund didn't speak. He leaned against the railing—not just resting his hands on it like before, but truly lying down, half his upper body against the railing—and looked down. He looked for a long time. He wasn't looking at anything specific. He was looking at everything.
Anna knew he was watching everything. She didn't interrupt.
Once, Edmund's hand flicked—pointing to a distant point to the southwest—a lighthouse in a settlement on the edge of the basin, too dark to see in daylight, but he remembered its location. He said nothing. He pointed, then lowered his hand.
When they returned, Anna helped him down the deck. His hand was much lighter than the one she had placed on her shoulder during her first flight.
After that day, he never said "go up and take a look" again. Anna would occasionally think of it—every time she thought of it, it was in the workshop, at a moment when Edmund's judgment was needed but he was still in bed—but she didn't bring it up. She didn't know why she didn't.
That was the last time.
She didn't know it was the last time. Neither of them knew. That's the nature of the "last time"—it's never recognized in the moment it happens.
Only later, much later, when looking back, did the slanting yellow light of that day, the extra half-second he spent gripping the railing, and the way he pointed southwest—all acquire their true weight.

Acoustic collapse
In the weeks following the flight, Anna's ears did something she only understood afterward: they archived the "openness of the sky."
Archiving doesn't mean forgetting—it means putting it in a drawer that's rarely opened. Occasionally, she would wake up at night and briefly hear that "one-way sound"—the wind passing by her and never returning—and then a few seconds later, Atheria's echo wall would be back in place. Each time she woke up like this, she didn't know what she was missing.
The echoes from the canyon aren't unpleasant. They're just different.
As Anna hung laundry out to dry on the platform in front of the Eagle's Nest—on an ordinary, uneventful afternoon—she heard a sound. An airship was passing over Atheria at an altitude of three thousand meters, heading south. The sound was faint, drifting down from above, its trajectory erratic against the cliff face before reaching her ears.
She looked up.
The airship flew overhead. A dark dot. She saw it from this angle—from the ground—the same angle she had seen countless times from the Eagle's Nest railings.
She kept looking at it even after her neck started to ache.
The airship finally disappeared at the edge of the southern cliff. Anna lowered her head. There were brief flashes of light in front of her for a few seconds—the sun was shining.
She continued hanging the clothes out to dry.

End frame
This module doesn't end on a single moment. It ends in a state without a moment: a 12-year-old girl, standing on the platform in front of the Eagle's Nest, holding wet clothes, her head just lowering from the angle of looking up.
She didn't know the sky had closed. She just noticed how quiet the house had been lately. Edmund hadn't been talking much lately. And lately she'd been sleeping more soundly at night—so soundly she couldn't hear the wind.
She has no idea that she won't be able to leave the ground for many years.
She will go to heaven again. But this time, Edmund will not be following her.


## M-19 · Two Hands (12 years old, Autumn)

**Deployment location:** Ep24 texture layer.
**Function:** The first beat of the world shutting down. Perception = the beginning of loss. Hearing Edmund's decline in sound.
**Rule:** The machine only acts as a carrier of emotion. The focus is on the shrinking relationship between Anna and Edmund, not on mechanic training.

---

The first thing Anna noticed wasn't that Edmund had slowed down. It was that his voice had changed.

When he did delicate work—tightening a tiny calibration screw, pinching a thin thread with pliers—there was a clean, definite sound between his fingers and tools: metal against metal, each movement crisp and precise. Anna grew up with that sound. It was part of the underlying chord of the Eagle's Nest workshop.

One autumn day that year—she couldn't remember the exact date—the sound came on again. After the first click, there was a very faint second click, one that shouldn't have been there. It was as if a finger had trembled, the tool had slid a tiny distance across the screw, and then clicked again.

Anna's ears caught that extraneous sound in that instant. She didn't turn her head. She continued with what she was doing. But her body knew.

Edmund knew Anna had heard him. He didn't look at her. He recalibrated the screw. The second time, there was no extra sound. He moved on to the next one.

From that day on, a new scene began to appear in the Eagle's Nest workshop: two people working on delicate tasks simultaneously. Edmund sat in his place, and Anna sat on a low stool beside him—not in an apprentice's position, but in a position where "she was also doing something." The work in her hands was assigned to her by Edmund; it wasn't difficult, but it required concentration. The two worked on their own tasks. Occasionally, Edmund would pause, shift his posture, and rest his hands on his knees. Anna didn't look at him. She could hear the sound of his hands on his knees—the slight rubbing of his knuckles against the fabric—and then he would pick up his tools again and continue.

The sounds of two hands overlapped. Anna's hands were young, fast, and not quite precise. Edmund's hands were slower and lighter, but each movement was perfectly placed. They formed an unstable duet in the workshop space—sometimes synchronized, sometimes out of sync.

Anna learned a great deal during this period. But she learned it not by "Edmund teaching her"—it was by "Edmund couldn't do it anymore, so she took over." She learned to tighten bolts not because of the course schedule, but because Edmund's hands were shaking.

---

## M-20 · Voice-Only (12 years old · Winter)

**Deployment location:** Ep24 texture layer.
**Function:** Edmund's final lesson. The teaching medium progresses from hand to sound. The sensory system undergoes its final upgrade.
**Rule:** This is not a "more advanced teaching method"—it's that Edmund's body no longer allows him to teach with his hands. The upgraded emotional context is loss.

---

When winter comes, Edmund can no longer leave the workshop.

It wasn't sudden. It was a gradual change—his visits to the workshop first went from daily to every other day, then from every other day to occasional. On good days, he would appear at the door, sit for a while, watch Anna and Joren work, and say a few words. On bad days, he was in bed. The blanket was up to his chest. His hands were under the blanket.

One day, Anna was fixing something—something she'd never fixed before, more complicated than anything she'd handled. She squatted on the floor, staring blankly at the pile of pipes and valves for a while. Edmund wasn't in the workshop. She went home and stood by Edmund's bedside.

"There's an unusual sound coming from the third pipe connection."

Edmund didn't sit up. His eyes opened. "What's wrong?"

"Dull. It should be crisp, but it's dull."

"There are two types of muffled sounds. Tap it once and listen. Use your knuckles. Lightly."

"I knocked. The sound doesn't dissipate."

"The washer is bulging." Edmund closed his eyes briefly. "Replace it. Third compartment in the spare parts box. Pay attention to the orientation—that washer isn't symmetrical; there's a notch pointing towards the water outlet."

Anna went back and exchanged it. It's fixed now.

From that day on, Edmund's teaching method changed. It wasn't about step-by-step instruction anymore. It wasn't about "watching how I do it." Instead, Anna would go to the site to observe, come back and describe what she saw, and Edmund would rely on sound to make judgments from his bed. He couldn't see the pipes and valves. He could only listen to what Anna said. Then he would say, "Try this."

The teaching medium shrank: from hands-on instruction → visual observation → pure audio. Not because it was better this way. It was because Edmund's body withdrew from the teaching layer by layer. In the end, only audio remained—his voice and Anna's voice as she described. The two voices exchanged at the bedside.

One day, Edmund said something. Anna was describing a strange noise from a water pump—"It sounds uneven, like two places are making noise at the same time, one high and one low, but not the same problem—"

Edmund interrupted her: "What you're hearing isn't things. What you're hearing is the relationship between things."

Anna paused for a moment.

"That high and low frequencies aren't two separate faults. It's a single fault in two different echo locations. Find the connection between them—the conduit, the resonant surface, the structural conduction path—and you'll find the source."

After Anna returned, she squatted next to the water pump and listened for a long time. The high frequencies came from a loose fixing bolt on top of the pump casing. The low frequencies came from a pipe underneath the pump casing that was in contact with the bracket. The connection between them was the pump casing itself—the bolt was loose, giving the pump casing a slight degree of freedom, and when it was running, the entire casing vibrated between the two contact points, with the high and low frequencies coming from the two ends of the same oscillation.

She tightened the bolt. Both sounds disappeared simultaneously.

One malfunction. Two symptoms. One relationship.

The last words Edmund taught her during her illness—"the relationship between things"—were the final driving force behind Anna's sensory system's upgrade from "hearing something" to "hearing the system."

But every time she thought of those words, she also thought of the hand hidden under the blanket.

---

## M-21 · Temperature Disappears (Age 13 · Late Winter)

**Deployment location:** Ep24.
**Function:** The death of Edmund.
**Rule:** This is not a climactic scene. The death occurs on an ordinary day.

---

Edmund was still the same person even in his good days.

He would sit on a chair by the workshop entrance for a while, basking in the sun. Winter sunlight was short, with only a two-hour window around noon. He would comment on the gait of passersby—"That person has bad knees; look, his ankle compensates for the angle when his right foot lands." He would take a sip of the black tin that Miriam offered and say, "Today's is more bitter than yesterday's; did you change the batch of beans?" He would see Anna come out of the workshop with her hands covered in oil, and say something—it was different each time—sometimes it was "Is that lubricant or your lunch?", sometimes he would just look at her and smile.

The good days made Anna think he was getting better. Then the bad days came back.

One day, Anna sat by Edmund's bedside. Edmund's hand emerged from under the blanket—not to do anything, but as he sometimes did on good days. Anna placed her hand on it.

Edmund's hands were lighter than she remembered. Her bones were more prominent. The calluses on her fingers were still there—calluses from thousands of wrench grips wouldn't disappear after a few months of inactivity—but the muscles beneath them were thinner.

Anna held his hand. He squeezed it back. Very gently.

He didn't say anything special that day. He might have, but Anna doesn't remember. What she remembers is the warmth of his hand.

She couldn't remember which day it was. One morning. Just like any other morning. The Atheria's morning bugle sounded as usual. The market was open as usual.

She held Edmund's hand. His hand was cold.

The disappearance of temperature is not like the disappearance of sound—there is no gradual weakening process. It's simply gone. The temperature that was there one second is gone the next. Anna doesn't know when it left. Maybe it was at night. Maybe it was in the early morning. In the hours she was away, the temperature quietly departed.

Miriam was beside her. Anna couldn't remember when Miriam had come in. Maybe she had been there all along.

Winter continues in Atheria.

---

## M-22 · Idle (13 years old · Spring)

**Deployment location:** Ep24.
**Function:** Habitual preparation to speak → No one is listening → That pause.
**Rule:** When you finish fixing something and look up to find no one to share it with—that pause is the heart of the scene, not the repair itself.

---

Anna went to the workshop the next day.

It wasn't because she was ready. It was because Edmund hadn't finished his last order: a ventilation valve assembly for a valley greenhouse. Edmund had drawn up the plans. The parts were ready. He hadn't had time to install them.

Anna followed the diagrams. Edmund's diagrams were very clear—small, neat handwriting, the direction of every pipe, the specifications of every connector, even the order of tightening the bolts were marked. She didn't need to think. She just had to follow them.

The hands are moving. The mind isn't.

It was afternoon when the installation was finished. Light streamed in through the workshop's side window, illuminating the grease on Anna's hands. She tightened the last bolt—three and a half turns, just as indicated on the blueprint—and then stretched.

She turned her head.

He was about to speak—his mouth was already open in shape—"Dad, it's all set up, look—"

There was no one in the workshop.

Edmund's spot was empty. The stool was there. The tools were there. The file he had last used lay across the edge of the workbench, untouched. But he was gone.

Anna's mouth closed. The "You see" remained unspoken.

This pause. This habitual pause of preparing to share something only to find the receiver no longer exists. It recurred in the days that followed—every time she finished fixing something, every time she heard something, every time her body automatically generated an urge to tell Edmund. The urge rose, reached her lips, hit an empty wall, and went back.

Anna didn't stop working. She completed each of Edmund's work orders one by one. Then she finished the ones Joren had assigned her. Then someone knocked on the door to ask about Edmund. Miriam said he had left. The person hesitated for a moment, and Anna said, "I'll go check on him."

She began to follow Edmund's route: Eagle's Nest Workshop, the valley floor, and the several fixed maintenance points in the middle of the cliff face. The same route Edmund took every day. She walked slower than Edmund—her legs were shorter, she had less experience, and she had to squat longer at each point. But she finished the route. She walked it again the next day. The third day.

She was wearing a red jacket.

---

## M-23 · Wearing a red jacket (Age 13, Spring)

**Deployment location:** Ep24, embedded within or immediately following the M-22.
**Rules:** This is not a ritual. It's not a moment. It's a cold morning when you need to wear a coat.

---

It was a few days after Edmund left, Anna wasn't sure. Maybe the third day. Maybe the fifth. It was cold in the morning. Was it spring or winter at Eagle's Nest altitude? The wind was blowing in from the canyon mouth, carrying the dampness of the river.

Anna felt cold as she put on her shoes by the door. She looked back at the coat hooks. Edmund's work coat hung on the far left; it was gray, thick, and smelled of oil. Miriam's shawl was in the middle. Anna's own thin coat hung on the right—it had been worn all winter and was faded from washing.

There was another color. Red. It hung next to Anna's coat.

She reached for her light jacket—her hand touched red. The fabric was thicker than her jacket. Dense. Windproof.

She took off her red jacket and put it on.

The sleeves fit perfectly. No need to roll them up. Edmund was ten when she said, "You'll fit when you grow a little longer." Now she's thirteen. She's grown enough.

She went out. The red hue shone brightly in the gray canyon in the early morning.

---

## M-24 · A thread has come loose (13 years old, spring)

**Deployment location:** Ep24.
**Function:** The existence of funerals and community. Edmund's death was not a dyad event—it occurred within a community.
**Rule:** Archeon's death is not an event—it's a thread coming loose.

---

The funeral was held in a communal open space at the bottom of the valley.

Atheria's funeral was short. An elder read something—Anna didn't hear it. It was something about the Loom, about a broken thread, the loom still standing, and the missing piece being filled by another thread. Anna stood in front. Miriam stood beside her. Miriam's hand was on Anna's back. It was the same hand she used to use to wipe Anna's sun-dried berry juice when she was a child.

Many people came. Some Anna knew, some she didn't. Some were from other settlements—those who had repaired things for Edmund. There was a man standing in the back row; Anna hadn't seen him before. He was tall, with old scars on his arms. He didn't come to the front. As he walked away, he nodded in Anna's direction. Anna didn't know who he was.

After the funeral, food was laid out on a long table. People ate. Some chatted. Some laughed—not out of disrespect, but in Archeon's way: the dead are gone, the living eat, and then go back to work. This isn't indifference. It's something learned over five hundred years: if you stay still too long, the loom will loosen.

An old woman tugged at Anna's sleeve as she passed by. It was a red sleeve. The old woman didn't say anything. She just tugged briefly and then let go. It was as if she was making sure Anna was still there.

Anna is here.

She walked back to the workshop. She carried nothing. Edmund's tools were where they belonged. She sat on Edmund's low stool—for the first time. Before, she had always sat on the one next to her. Now she sat on his. The wood of the stool, worn smooth by his weight over a decade, had a slight indentation in the middle, a shape shaped by his weight.

Anna's weight wasn't enough to fill that dent.

---

## M-25 · Six months (13 years old · Spring to Summer)

**Deployment Location:** Compressed transition between M-22/M-24 and M-26.
**Function:** Spring energy storage section for the Lia event. Surface is normal, but there is pressure buildup underneath.
**Rules:** It doesn't need to be long—half a page to a full page. It's not about the scene, it's about compressed time.

---

Spring is over. And then comes the beginning of summer.

Anna accomplished a great deal in those six months: repaired over a dozen pieces of equipment, replaced countless gaskets and seals, and learned to use Edmund's high stool to reach previously inaccessible access ports on the middle layer of the cliff face. Every morning, she would put on her red jacket, walk Edmund's route, and work on Edmund's work orders—later assigned to her by Joren. Her hands became increasingly steady. Her judgment became increasingly quick. People began to say, "The Freedman's daughter has good hands."

She didn't cry.

Miriam noticed. But she didn't say anything. Miriam herself was doing similar things—going to the market, cooking, and seeing people as usual. Their days in the Eagle's Nest were quiet. They said what needed to be said, and kept quiet about what shouldn't be said. Anna didn't know if Miriam cried at night. Miriam didn't know if Anna did.

Anna walked around Edmund's tool rack. Not intentionally—her route naturally avoided that corner. Above the tool rack was the spare parts box. Joren had mentioned once, "There are sealing rings in the spare parts box; get them if you need them." Anna agreed. Then she didn't go. Not because she forgot.

Sometimes, after Anna finishes fixing something, she'll stand there for a moment. Not long—just a few seconds. Her lips will move slightly, as if she's preparing to say something. Then her face will return to a flat expression. She'll pick up her tools and move on to the next spot.

Joren had seen this. He didn't ask about it.

Someone brought soup. Not every day. Every few days. An old lady Anna couldn't recall the name of. Miriam accepted it. Anna didn't think about who brought the soup when she drank it.

Anna appears to be a perfectly normal person. She works on time, eats on time, and her repair skills are visibly improving. If you didn't know her, you would think she was a capable, quiet, thirteen-year-old apprentice mechanic.

The spring was stretched for six months.

---

## M-26 · Lia Incident (13 years old, summer)

**Deployment location:** Ep45 full unfold / Arc 3 peel.
**Preliminary Status:** Refer to M-25. Anna has been operating for six months. Surface appears normal, but there is internal pressure.

---

Joren's comment from a few days ago that "there's a sealing ring in the spare parts box" hasn't been followed—not because she forgot, but because the spare parts box is above Edmund's tool rack, and she's been walking around that tool rack for the past six months.

Anna moved a low stool, stood on it, and opened the spare parts box. The sealing rings were inside, neatly sorted by specifications, placed in the small compartments marked by Edmund. His handwriting—small and neat—was on every label.

As she picked up the sealing ring, she bumped into something. Edmund's wrench. Not the workshop's communal one—it was his own. He'd used it for over a decade. The handle had been worn into a specific shape by his hands: a groove where he gripped it with his right hand, and a smooth indentation where his thumb would rest. It had been honed by tens of thousands of grips and releases.

She put her hand into the groove.

The weight matched his memory perfectly. The metal's temperature in the afternoon light was close to the temperature of Edmund's palm when he was alive. Maybe it was this. Maybe it was nothing at all. The spring had been taut for half a year, and it was here.

Anna slammed the wrench down on the workbench with all her might. —No, she meant to. But she was standing on a low stool, and her strength and angle were both off. The wrench flew out of her hand. It wasn't an intentional throw; the force of the slam was too great, and she lost her grip.

The stool was right next to the spare parts rack—because the spare parts box was originally above the rack, you had to stand on the stool to reach it. The wrench, after slipping from my hand, didn't fly far—about an arm's length—before hitting a support rod in the middle of the spare parts rack. The angle was tricky, but the distance was very short.

[PATCH-08 Improved Physical Reliability]
Original: The wrench "flew over the workbench and over Edmund's tool rack"—implying a flight distance of several meters.
The 13-year-old girl's explosive force while on a low stool caused the wrench to hit the support pole several meters away precisely—too low.
New version: The stool was originally next to the spare parts rack (because the spare parts box was above the rack).
The wrench flew only an arm's length after slipping from his grasp. Close-range impact + anchor bolts that had been loose for six months = the collapse was reasonable.
The weight of the causal chain shifts from "how far the wrench flew" to "how long the bolt was loose"—
This reinforces the systemic factor of "maintenance void after Edmund's departure."

The anchor bolts of this spare parts rack were last inspected when Edmund was alive. Six months after he passed away, no one took over the inspection. It wasn't Anna's responsibility—it was a gaping hole in the entire Eagle's Nest maintenance schedule. Edmund was gone, and his responsibilities wouldn't automatically be taken over. The bolts had loosened.

The wrench didn't hit the most critical spot. But for a set of anchors that had been loose for six months, any spot was critical. The scaffold tilted. Heavy components from the top two layers—cast iron couplings, spare bearing housings, and half a roll of sealant—slipped down. The outer side of the scaffold faced the workshop's outer wall. The outer wall wasn't a load-bearing wall; it was a later addition of lightweight partitions. The heavy components smashed through the partitions and fell onto the outer walkway.

There are people on the plank road.

Lia, seven years old. She has a congenital chronic lung problem and walks slower than other children. She happened to be on the boardwalk—perhaps on her way somewhere, perhaps just walking. Her slow pace meant she didn't have time to dodge. Cast iron couplings and fragments fell on her.

Left rib fracture. Lung contusion—an acute injury compounded by a pre-existing chronic condition.

---

Anna stood at the entrance of the workshop.

Her hands were empty—the wrench was gone. She saw bolts and half-rolls of sealant scattered on the walkway. She saw the skewed angle of the spare parts rack. She saw the straight line of cause and effect running from her hand to Lia.

She saw a seven-year-old child lying in the rubble.

Anna's senses were perfectly sharp at that moment. She saw everything. Every single detail. She could see the straight line of cause and effect running from her hand to Lia.

---

The older generation later handled the situation the way Archeon did—instead of assigning blame to any one person, they looked for loopholes in the system.

Their first question was: When was the last time the anchor bolts for this spare parts rack were inspected? The answer pointed to Edmund—after he left, the maintenance he was responsible for wasn't reassigned. This was a systemic flaw in The Loom, not anyone's fault. The older generation took the blame.

Then they looked at where the wrench landed. They reconstructed the trajectory: from Anna's position, at that angle, with that force, the wrench didn't "fall." It was thrown out forcefully.

What Joren said to Anna—not as an interrogation, but as a statement—was: "The shelf problem was a mistake on all sides. The bolts went unchecked for six months. But the wrench—that wasn't an accident."

Anna did not deny it.

Joren was silent for a moment. He didn't ask her to explain.

---

Miriam。

She looked at the scene. She listened to Anna—not an explanation, Anna didn't know how to explain. She simply recounted what had happened. The wrench. The spare parts rack. Lia.

Miriam said, "You've been pretending you're okay for six months."

This was the first time Anna cried in Miriam's arms.

Miriam's hand was on Anna's back. It was the same hand that used to wipe Anna's back with sunberry juice when they were little.

---

## M-27 · Three-second pause (13 years old, after summer)

**Deployment location:** Closely following the M-26.
**Function:** The birth of Anna's core mechanisms. Represented throughout the entire book.

---

This isn't a flashback of psychological trauma. It's a mechanic's physical memory. Her hand remembers how the force went from gripping to letting go. Every time a similar force accumulates in her hand—tightening a particularly tight bolt, moving a heavy part, or even slamming a door shut—her hand hesitates.

That hesitation, slowly, extended to all decisions. Not just the strength in her hands. It was everything that "pushed her forward."

Anna started doing one thing whenever an impulse arose: stop, for about three seconds, and ask herself a question.

Was the hand that pushed me the wind or myself?

If it's the wind—an external, real necessity—follow it.
If it's your own impulse—an internal one that might be driven by grief, guilt, or fear—wait.

This mechanism wasn't taught to her by anyone. No one sat down and said, "You should do this." It was Edmund's methodology—the "stop, listen, then do" by the river stone—a spontaneous transformation after trauma. Edmund taught curiosity-driven pauses. Anna adopted fear-driven pauses. The same action, completely different motivations.

[Design Note] The three-second pause makes Anna safer, but it also makes her slower. Over the next six years, this mechanism will be both her greatest strength and her greatest weakness—the same thing. It prevents her from hurting people, but it also makes her hesitant to trust her first instinct when quick decisions are needed. This insight is left for the reader to piece together through the main behavioral patterns; prose will not summarize it.

---

## M-28 · Visiting Lia empty-handed (13 years old, autumn/winter)

**Deployment Location:** Arc 3 Lia line peel material.
**Function:** Establishes the foundation for Anna and Lia's relationship. Anna chooses the starting point for the distance.

---

Lia's ribs were healing. Lung contusions heal slower than bones—her congenital condition complicated the repair of acute injuries. For a time, she lived in that ventilated room at the bottom of the valley, a place Anna didn't know who had arranged—perhaps Miriam, perhaps her elders.

Anna visited her.

The first time she went, she stood outside for a long time. She didn't know what to bring. She had nothing in her hands. What should a mechanic bring when visiting someone she'd hurt? An apology? Food? Flowers? Atheria doesn't have these customs. Not that they don't—it's just that Anna didn't know what to bring. In the end, she brought nothing. She went in empty-handed.

Lia was in bed. Smaller than Anna remembered. The seven-year-old looked only five, huddled under the blanket. Her eyes were on Anna. There was no fear. No welcome. Just looking.

Anna didn't know what to say.

She stood by the bed. Lia looked at her. Anna looked at Lia. There was a causal chain between the two of them—from the workshop to the walkway, from the wrench to the cast iron coupling, from Anna's hand to Lia's left rib—this line stretched between them, invisible but known to both of them.

Anna sat down on the stool beside the bed. She didn't say "I'm sorry"—not because she didn't want to, but because those two words weren't enough. And she couldn't explain why they weren't enough.

Lia spoke first.

"Your jacket is very red."

Anna glanced down at herself. The red jacket. She'd forgotten she was still wearing it.

"Um."

"My mom's scarf is also red, but not as red as yours."

This is a seven-year-old doing what a seven-year-old does—comparing what's in front of her with what she knows. Lia isn't talking about what happened that day. She's talking about colors.

Anna sat for a while. Not long. She didn't know how long she should stay. Lia started coughing—a congenital cough, unrelated to her injury—Anna stood up, wanting to do something, but not knowing what to do, and finally she retreated to the doorway. There was an adult at the door—probably the person looking after Lia—giving her a look that said, "You can leave now."

Anna is gone.

She went a few more times after that. Each time she went empty-handed. Each time she didn't know what to say. Each time Lia would say something unrelated to that day—about the bugs she saw in the room, about the soup being too hot, about the leaves on the tree outside the window turning yellow. Anna listened. Occasionally she would respond with a word.

Every time she left, she felt she should have done more. But she didn't know what "more" meant.

In winter, Lia could go out. Short distances. She still walked slowly—slower than before. Anna sometimes saw her from afar on the boardwalk. A red jacket and a slowly walking child were separated by the entire length of the boardwalk. Anna didn't go over.

She began to maintain a distance. Not intentionally—or perhaps intentionally, but she wouldn't admit it. Close contact had hurt a child. Her body had welded "closeness" and "danger" together. A three-second pause controlled her hands. But not her feet—her feet began to automatically choose the farther path.

This distance will only grow further and further over the next six years.

### Anna-Lia · Six-year interval (14-19 years old baseline)

After Anna flew away at the age of 14, she returned to Atheria every three to four days. Lia lived at the bottom of the valley. Anna lived in the eagle's nest on the upper cliff. The physical distance was not short—from the eagle's nest to the bottom of the valley, one had to take a lift and traverse different passageways. Anna's route back was: airport → eagle's nest → valley maintenance point → home. She didn't need to pass through the area where Lia lived.

However, it's unrealistic to expect complete zero interaction within Atheria's 30,000-person community.

A more accurate baseline:

```
Anna flew away when Anna was 14 (Lia was 8). On her return, she occasionally saw Lia in the distance on the boardwalk.
A slow-moving child is at the bottom of a valley. He doesn't actively try to reach it.

15-16 years old (Lia 9-10 years old): Nodding in the crowd at a festival gathering. At the same stall on market day.
We brushed past each other. The distance between us—knowing each other but having no reason to speak.

At age 17 (Lia was 11), Miriam once mentioned, "Lia is doing better lately."
Anna didn't press further. Miriam didn't say much either.
There was a silent consensus between the two women: not to delve into the topic.

18-year-old Anna (Lia was 12) returned to Atheria to perform maintenance and repairs at the cliffside workshop.
While having lunch at the Common House at the bottom of the valley, Lia sat at the other end of the long table.
There were five or six people between them. Anna saw Lia coughing—
That short, controlled cough. The cough of gravidarum.
Anna's finger paused briefly on the rim of the bowl. Then she continued eating.
They did not speak.

19 years old (Lia 13 years old), Arc 2 begins. Baseline temperature:
We lived in the same canyon for six years, knew each other but never really talked.
Anna knew Lia's name, knew she walked slowly, and knew she was still alive.
Lia knew Anna was the pilot in the red jacket.
There is a causal connection between the two that has never been spoken of.
```

The temperature was just right—cool enough—there was tension when they first appeared named in Arc 2. Yet warm enough—there was no hostility between them. Only Anna's one-sided guilt and distance.

[The six years on Lia's side]
Lia has grown up in these six years. From age 7 to 13. Her ribs have long since healed.
; polio is chronic—it doesn't get better or suddenly worsen (until it gets worse).
Her life at the bottom of the Atheria Valley consisted of: walking slowly, observing much, and being rejected by workshops.
They developed a mindset that "everything is ultimately a lever."
Her attitude towards Anna wasn't resentment—it was closer to a watchful neutrality.
She knew the man in the red jacket. She knew he came back every three or four weeks.
She didn't go over to her. Anna didn't come over.
This symmetrical distance is the result of the choices made by each of the two individuals.
Anna chose guilt-driven avoidance, but what did Lia choose? (Open question)
(Resolved between Ep65-67: Lia knows. Always knew. Distance is an informed choice.)

## Critical Reference Chart for Childhood Perception Stages (for reference when writing Ep22-24)

| Age | Global radius | Open channels | Closed channels | Key moment | Emotional tone |
|------|---------|-----------|-----------|-----------|---------|
| 3-6 | Eagle's Nest ±50m | Sight (guess the time by sky color), Touch (lizard/sunberry/wind), Smell (engine oil = dad) | Mechanical diagnosis, system understanding | M-01 three-part safety signal, solo observation first, sharing later | Safety, curiosity, laughter, sweetness |
| 6-8 | Eagle's Nest → Basin | Touch → Auditory Bridging (M-06 River Stone), Rhythm Perception (Waterwheel), Social Observation (Miriam Line), Spatial Hierarchy (M-05 Layering) | Mechanical Diagnosis, System Understanding | The function of M-06 is not "learning to listen" but "sharing the joy of discovery" | Discovery, Rhythm, Resonance |
| 8-9 | Canyon → Above → Nearby Outpost | Aerial Experience (M-09b's first flight), Macro Vision, Soundscape Integration (M-14 Chord = "The Temper of a Cliff"), Environmental Forecast (Miriam Line) | Mechanical Diagnosis, Independent Maintenance | M-09b = First time seeing the world from above with Edmund. Preparation for M-18 Bond Peak | Opening, Flying, Conspiracy |
| 9-10 | Basin → Nearby outside the basin | The machine as a "living being with a temper" (M-13 airship breathing, "it is breathing"), full-channel integration (M-18's all ground sensory memories arrive simultaneously from the air = bond peak) | Machine diagnosing language (19-year-old language is not 9-year-old language) | The way the door opens is natural extension, not teaching. Machine ≤15% attention | Independence, discovery, two-way |
| 12-13 | Sky → Ground → Workshop → Bedside | Understanding system relationships (M-20 "Relationships between things"), perception of bodily decline, disappearance of temperature (M-21) | — | Narrowing is drift, not event. Perception ability hasn't disappeared, but the object has narrowed | Contraction, final, pause |

**Constraints:** Edmund axis = reading inanimate objects (add it when she gets there herself), Miriam axis = reading living objects (casual/brief/no explanation). Edmund is not a poet. Childhood red = red clay dye (botanical red is left to Skyleaf). The machine never exceeds 15-20% attention—exceeding that and it falls into a gravity well.

]

## I. How does the door open?
Approximately eight months after the Lia incident.

Anna's days took on a new rhythm. Joren adjusted her work scope—she assisted his maintenance team, no longer handling large equipment alone. This wasn't punishment. It was a reasonable arrangement, one that Anna herself agreed to. Her hands could still work, but something new had grown between them—a hesitation she couldn't yet name. A three-second pause had begun to form within her; each time the pressure in her hands approached a certain threshold, she would briefly pull back from the action.

Her ears did something she hadn't noticed during those eight months. They began to grow impatient with the echoes from the valley floor.

It wasn't that she disliked it. It was something more physical—like wearing shoes that were too small. In the months following M-18b, her ears were forced back from "one-way sound" to an "echo wall," and she thought she had gotten used to it. But getting used to it and adapting are not the same thing. Some mornings she would wake up with a second or two of "emptiness" in her ears—not silence, but the kind of openness that only exists at three thousand meters altitude, where a sound is spoken once and then gone—and then the echo wall of Atheria would rise again, and the "emptiness" would disappear. She didn't know what she was missing.

One afternoon—a day completely unrelated to anything else—she was hanging laundry on the platform in front of the Eagle's Nest. A blimp passed overhead, its engine whirring against the cliff face as it drifted down. She looked up. Then she took a step toward Skyport.

It's not a decision. It's a reflex. Like when you're six years old and lying on a river rock, your body puts your ear to the rock before your brain does—your body gets there first.

She stopped. A three-second pause. Then she continued hanging the clothes. No flights that day.

But her body remembered that step.

That early spring, a coastal settlement on the West Coast needed to ship a shipment from Atheria—breeding starter cultures from Canyon, several boxes of copper leaf seedlings cultivated in the Atheria valley's greenhouse, and a replacement valve set made by Joren. It was a standard freight schedule. Joren had originally sent someone else, but that person had a family emergency. They were short-handed to accompany the shipment.

"Send Anna," Joren told the person in charge of scheduling. "She knows the specifications of that valve assembly; she can help with the installation when she gets there."

No one hesitated. It was a very small task.

There was only one problem: there was no road between Atheria and the settlement. It was separated by a river valley carved into the central plain and dozens of kilometers of hills. The only way was by airship.

Had Anna ever ridden in an airship before? She flew with Edmund when she was a child. But in the year following the Lia incident, she didn't leave Atheria. Her world shrank to the fifty-meter boardwalk between the Eagle's Nest workshop and the valley floor.

That morning, when she arrived at the airport with a small bag on her back—not a tool bag, just the manifest needed for the cargo and a few changes of clothes—a Class II cargo airship awaited her. The captain's name was Hagen. His brown face was covered in fine lines from the wind, he had a long scar on his arm, and his beard looked like a used steel wool pad.

"From Freedman's?"

"yes."

"Does your mom know you're here?"

"She signed it."

Are you seasick?

"have no idea."

Hagen's eyebrows twitched. "Okay. Get on. Don't throw up on my deck."

---

Anna stood by the starboard railing during takeoff. Hagen told her to stand there—"New pilots always want to look outside the first time, and it's less windy over there."

One by one, the mooring lines were untied. The airship rose from its berth like a bubble from the seabed. Quietly. Anna's stomach sank, then settled. The airport platform shrank beneath her feet. The two cliffs of the Atheria Valley receded to either side like two doors opening.

The moment she crossed the crest of the hill—the echo wall vanished. The sound became one-way. The valley reverberation that had filled her ears for over a year was suddenly emptied, like someone had pulled a cork from a bottle. The wind passed by her and kept going and going until she could hear nothing at all.

She didn't know why her eyes were welling up with tears. One of the crew members unloading the cargo glanced at her but didn't say anything.

Then--

world.

This wasn't a bird's-eye view of the basin—the one she'd seen countless times from the Eagle's Nest railing. It was a world beyond the basin. The land unfolded towards the canyon mouth—golden-green vibratoforests stretched along the horizon, shimmering in the wind, but not a uniform blanket: distant low hills undulated, casting long shadows between the fields; several basalt terraces rose isolated from the plain like dark islands floating on a golden sea; further still, shallow valleys sliced ​​the land into patches of green of varying shades. The whole land seemed to breathe, but not a calm breath—it was undulating, rhythmic, layered. Further still, the Frontier City skyline loomed in the morning mist—a few CI-era pylon peaks emerged from the undulating terrace matrix, enveloped by five-hundred-year-old growth layers, appearing from this distance like a low, moss-covered mountain range, its high points indistinct, nestled on its own raised terrace base.

Anna gripped the railing tightly. Not out of fear, but because it was too big.

"It's always like this the first time," Hagen said from the cockpit. The wind muffled his voice, but she could hear him laughing. "You won't feel so big anymore once you've flown enough."

"I don't want to fly enough," Anna said.

Hagen didn't answer. But he later mentioned the trip to others: "Most beginners, on their first flight, react by gripping tighter and pulling back. The Freedman girl let go and leaned forward."

---

## Part Two: Three Days on the Coast

The journey to the coastal settlement wasn't long—a little over three hours. Anna stood on the deck most of the time. Hagen didn't let her sit idle—there were no "passengers" on the airship, only "crew members who hadn't yet learned how to work." He had her help with hauling in the cables, passing tools, and checking the barometer. Anna did it. Her hands felt a strange ease as she performed these unfamiliar tasks—because the cables weren't wrenches. The cables wouldn't fly off and hit anyone. They were only there to hold things in place.

That place is completely different from Atheria.

There are no canyons. No cliffs. It sits on gentle hills facing Azure Main. The air is salty and damp with earth. The fields stretch to the horizon—not the meticulously planned terraces at the bottom of canyons like those in Atheria, but vast, luxuriously flat farmland, the wheat ears swaying like golden waves in the sea breeze. The airport isn't on the cliff face but atop a grassy slope, the airships moored to anchor piles on the ground like a herd of giant buffalo grazing in the meadow.

**The first thing that struck her was the sound.**

It's not quiet—it has its own voice. But it lacks the echo of a canyon. In Atheria, every sound bounces off the cliffs, creating reverberation and layering, resulting in a three-dimensional, rich sound. Here, the sound is flat. It dissipates as soon as it leaves the ground, like water splashed on sand.

Anna's sound map almost completely fails here.

It took her most of the day to adjust. By the next morning, she could begin to read the chords of this place—not the dense symphony of Atheria, but a more expansive, wind- and sea-based plains soundscape. The direction of the wheat field's waves indicated the wind direction. The speed of the waves indicated the wind speed. The occasional "still bands" in the waves indicated that the wheat was shorter than the surrounding area, possibly due to lack of water or soil problems.

No one taught her these things. This was an extension of Atheria's perception that grew out of itself in a completely different environment.

The valve assembly was quickly settled—locals took over the installation, Anna helped check the specifications, and confirmed everything was correct. She spent the rest of her time walking around the settlement. Not working. Just walking.

She walked through wheat fields swaying in the sea breeze. She passed through a market completely different from the ones in Atheria—flatter, more spread out, with stalls spaced further apart, because here there was no need for the crowds of the canyon. She smelled flour, dried grass, and the salty, fishy aroma of seaweed baked by the sun in the distance. In the communal dining hall, she ate something she'd never tasted before—a soft, fluffy bread made with locally ground flour, dipped in a thick root soup.

An old woman who runs a bakery—everyone calls her "Aunt Flour" because she is always covered in a layer of white flour from head to toe—serves this bowl of soup to her without saying a word.

"Thank you... but I have nothing to offer—"

"I can use the starter culture you brought from Atheria tomorrow. The bread you ate was made from last year's batch." Aunt Flour's tone was as if she were commenting on the nice weather. "Don't be shy. Eat."

That evening, as Anna helped Aunt Flour move the canyon starter culture into the cool storage room, she sat on the grassy slope of the airport. Azure Main, bathed in the twilight light, became a plane of deep blue and orange-gold. Above, there were no canyon walls, only the unobstructed sky. Betelgeuse stretched out to the west, much larger than it appeared from within the canyon—unbound by cliffs, it filled half the sky. Rivers of purple and rose light stretched from the horizon to the zenith.

**Different places have different smells.** Atheria smells of rocks, metal, and engine oil. Here, it smells of flour, salty winds, and dried grass. The people in these two places live on the same planet, speak the same language, and eat the same wheat introduced by terraform—but their daily lives are two completely different sensory worlds.

This is what the pilot sees. Not from a fixed point—but moving between different points, each stop offering a different taste, different light, and a different person bringing you a bowl of soup.

That evening, Anna made a decision. She didn't know how to fly an airship yet, but she knew she had to learn.

---

## III. Learning to Fly

### Hagen's Rules

After returning to Atheria, Anna went to find Hagen.

"Teach me to fly."

Hagen was repairing the worn sails on the deck. He looked up at her.

"How old are you?"

"fourteen."

"The official document will be submitted on the 15th."

"I can learn first."

What did your mom say?

She said she has no objection if you're willing to teach.

Hagen stood up. He was a head and a half taller than her. He held up three fingers.

"My rules. First, I say stop, and you stop. Whether you think you're right or wrong. You can argue afterward—I like arguing with people—but in the air, what I say is the law."

"Second, if you're seasick, wipe yourself off. Then get back to work."

"Third," his expression turned serious. "An airship isn't a workshop. In a workshop, if you make a mistake, the worst that can happen is a pump explodes or a wall collapses. On an airship, if you make a mistake, you and everyone on board will fall from two thousand meters. I've heard about the mistakes you've made on land. I don't care. That's a land matter. But on my airship, think for three seconds before you make a mistake: Will your decision cause someone else to fall? Think it through before you act. If you're not sure, come ask me."

Anna nodded.

"Sunrise tomorrow. Airport berth B3. Bring your own gloves."


### From Deck to Steer

For the first three months, Anna didn't touch the steering wheel. She did deck work: taking up lines, checking airbags, repairing sails, and monitoring temperatures. Hagen's method of teaching flight was strikingly similar to Edmund's method of teaching perception—first letting you feel with your hands, then telling you the name of the principle.

Anna's hands quickly developed new calluses—not the kind from tightening bolts in the workshop, but from the friction of thick cables. She learned to maintain her balance in the wind. The airship's deck tilted and swayed slightly in the air currents, like standing on a live fish. For the first two weeks, she walked like she was drunk.

But she learned faster than most beginners. Not because of mechanic skills—it was because her body already had nine years of sensory foundation. She could "hear" the way the wind blew through the deck trusses—winds from different directions creating different frequency patterns within the metal structure. She could feel the changes in airbag pressure on her skin—tiny gradients in temperature and humidity. She knew the deck's tendency to tilt—it was the same balance system she'd used for over a decade walking on the Atheria cliff walkway, only the frame of reference was changing.

Hagen thought he was teaching a novice. He was actually giving someone with nine years of sensory experience a new domain. Not a mechanical one. It was nine years of experience of listening, seeing, and living in the world with one's body.

Three months later, on a calm afternoon—a standard flight, with clear skies—Hagen called her over.

"Sit there. Hold on."

The steering wheel felt alive in her hands. A constant, slight tremor—a combined feedback from the wind, airbags, and hull structure transmitted through the control cables. Not the pulse of a machine. It was the symphony of the entire ship, the air around it, and the earth beneath it—all of these things.

"You felt it?" Hagen asked from the side.

"...It's talking to me."

"say what?"

Anna closed her eyes. The wind was coming from the left front, at an angle of about twenty degrees. The hull had a slight tendency to roll to the right. The rudder response was gentle, without any sudden changes.

"Everything is normal. But the port side is drifting a little bit to starboard. One or two degrees."

Hagen was silent for five seconds. Then he said:

"You already knew how to listen when you arrived. I don't know who taught you. But the sky is bigger than a machine. You could hear it."

[Design Note] Hagen sensed Anna's perceptual foundation but didn't know its source. The "listening" taught by Edmund and the "listening" taught by Hagen are expressions of the same perceptual system in two domains—but this connection only exists within Anna's body and the reader's cognition, and is not externalized by any character. Anna's choice to fly was not abandoning what Edmund gave her—it was taking what Edmund gave her to the sky. This understanding is only conveyed to us in Episode 25 through the present-time flight scene, not explicitly revealed in the backstory.

That afternoon, with Hagen's hand hovering over hers—not touching, just there like a safety net—Anna took her first flight. Forty minutes. Straight line. Smooth.

Her hands trembled as she handed the steering wheel back to Hagen. Not from nervousness. It was something deeper than joy. As a child, when flying kites, she had felt the wind as a dance partner. Now she knew that was just the prelude.

### Assessment

One month after my fifteenth birthday. Formal pilot apprenticeship assessment.

Archeon has no flight school. A civilization of one million people doesn't have the extra resources to maintain formal educational institutions. Pilot training relies entirely on an apprenticeship system—you fly enough hours with an experienced pilot, learn enough skills, and once your mentor deems you ready, he takes you to take the exam.

The assessment takes place in Frontier City—where there are the most pilots, the most complete airport facilities, and an informal judging panel called "The Murder." Six or seven veteran pilots with over twenty years of flying experience sit in the airport teahouse every day, drinking tea and commenting on every airship that arrives or departs. There are no uniforms, no documents, no offices. But in The Loom, if "The Murder" nods, you're a qualified pilot. If they shake their heads, no captain will hire you.

The assessment was a real flight. Real route, real cargo, real weather.

Anna's assigned route that day was to a medium-sized settlement called Iron Vine—located in a deep valley on the edge of the central plain. Approaching the berth required precise, low-speed turns in the narrow valley. The "crow" sent to observe was an elderly woman named Savona—in her sixties, with a full head of silver hair and skin as black as if it had been smoked. She was said to be the only person to have survived flying backwards out of the Serpent's Tooth Falcon Gate storm in her youth. She sat in the corner of the cabin without uttering a single word.

The valley bend was crucial. The crosswind was stronger than expected. Anna completed a 90-degree turn in less than 500 meters, controlling the descent rate and dealing with the accelerating crosswinds. Her hands remained steady on the steering wheel—she wasn't thinking about theory, she was thinking about the kite. As a child, flying a kite in the updrafts of the canyon, the string taut in her hand, the instant the wind changed direction wasn't a reaction of the brain, but of the hand.

Docked. Smooth and precise.

Savona stood up. She walked over. Without saying a word, she extended her root-like hand, patted Anna on the shoulder, and turned and left.

Hagen leaned closer and whispered, "She took your picture. That means you passed."

Anna took three seconds to understand the sentence. Then her knees buckled. She sat on the deck, looked up at the narrow strip of sky above the Iron Vine Valley, and smiled.

---

## IV. Days on the Flight (Ages 16-18)

### The world is a sensory map

After passing the assessment, Anna's world transformed from a canyon into a net.

She flew most of the routes as co-pilot. The western route was easy—plain terrain and steady westerly winds. The southern route crossed the northern edge of the Orun Plateau—the airflow was like a pot of boiling water. The eastern hilly region was a different story—remote settlements with only a few hundred people, and berths were just two stakes and a rope in the grass.

Each settlement was a different sensory experience. Anna began to build a map within her body—not a flight path, but a **taste map**.

**Coppergate**'s first impression is dryness. The air carries a sun-baked, dusty smell—a thin layer of ochre-red dust carried by the wind from the Orun Plateau to the south, settling in this gateway town for north-south trade. Cargo density is higher than elsewhere—it's not uncommon to see three or four airships docked at the airport simultaneously. The noise is deafening, but efficient; the loading and unloading pace is as rapid as drumming. Anna tastes salted jerky brought from further south for the first time here—it's so salty she drinks three cups of water, but it has a smoky, mineral flavor she's never experienced in Atheria.

**A big city** is about density. Anna understood for the first time what a "big city" meant—not size, but the density of choices. In Atheria, you only have two or three options for lunch. Here, you can stand in the center of the market and see fifteen different kinds of food steaming hot. The layers of sounds kept her up all night.

The small outposts in the eastern hills are quiet and vast. Sounds scatter across the grasslands in a way similar to coastal settlements, but drier—lacking the low-frequency undertones of the sea, the distant wind is the baseline. Some outposts have only a few dozen households. The arrival of an airship is an event. Everyone comes out to watch. Children chase after its shadow. Anna stays in these places for two or three days, not because of a lot of work—but because the flight intervals are that long. While waiting for her return flight, she eats and chats with the locals, is taken to see newborn horned lambs, and is given a jar of homemade brew by an old woman.


### The prototype of the shuttle man

What does Anna do on the flight path?

**Transporting goods.** The most basic thing. The Gustress carries cargo. But the cargo isn't abstract cargo—it's a barrel of salted fish from a small town in the southern canyon to Atheria, a basket of fresh seafood from the West Coast, a letter, a packet of seeds, and a repaired tool brought back from a small outpost request. Each item connects to a person and a need. Anna knows who salted the fish. Who the seeds are for. She is the human bridge between these goods.

**Bringing Messages.** Airships arrive carrying two things: cargo and news. Anna arrives at a settlement, bringing news from three places: who had a baby; which flight path had been affected by recent strong winds and schedule changes; the Thread Festival being moved up two weeks this year; and the poor yellowhead harvest on the Iron Vine. This news was as valuable as the cargo—a council might adjust its food reserves based on a casual remark from Anna.

**Look.** This is Anna's unique value. She arrives at a settlement, takes a stroll. No rush. Not looking for faults—she's sensing the rhythm. The canal flows faster than last time; it's rained upstream. The market is quieter; something's affecting trade. People's faces are either tighter or looser. The stone bridge feels half a degree warmer underfoot—in short, without pausing: "Spring is deepening." Then she continues walking.

She carried these observations to the next stop. Not in the form of a report—but casually mentioned over a long-table lunch. "Coppergate has had a lot of salt transiting through this year, and they'd like to swap some of your Canyon brews for it. Interested?" "The irrigation at that little outpost on the east side seems to be having some problems. If anyone goes there next time, could you check it out?" This casual flow of information was the lifeblood of The Loom.

She wasn't just reading the water and the wind. Once, while docked at Coppergate, Anna noticed a pottery vendor at the dockside market had moved his stall three steps further inward from where he'd last been. She didn't stop. But as she walked, her body began to adjust: the dockworkers were wearing thicker clothes than before; the tent ropes across the square were tauter than usual; an old man mending fishing nets in the harbor wasn't sitting in his usual spot—he'd moved to the sheltered corner against the wall. Three people, three subtle adjustments in behavior, all pointing to the same thing. After returning to the Gustatory, Anna adjusted the departure time for the next day. She didn't explain why to anyone. The weather changed in the early hours of the next morning.

[Design Notes] This beat is a living extension of the M-08's "Birds Know Before the Sky." Miriam's "reading living things" and Edmund's "reading dead things" are two parallel lines. In the flying era, Edmund's line was fully extended (rudder wheel sensing, airbag pressure, sound maps), and Miriam's line here adds a prose layer—Anna doesn't just read bird behavior to predict the weather; she reads human behavior patterns. The "snapshot of the old man's eye" in the M-12 market day when he was nine years old was unconscious; the same set of perceptions at seventeen had been internalized into work habits. Joren later said, "You look at the water, the wind, people, and their expressions"—this prose is evidence of that statement.

Does Anna not fix things? Yes, she does. But fixing things is never her reason for going to a place—it's something she happens to encounter while she's in a place. It's like helping someone who has fallen down on the street—helping someone isn't your purpose in going out, but you did do it.

[Design Constraints] Anna's mechanic work in settlements (ages 16-18) should account for ≤10% of screen time. If a prose sequence spends more than 3 sentences describing her crouching down to fix something, that's too much. Repairing is a node in the transit, not the destination. Her narrative rhythm should be: fly—land—walk—look—speak—fly. Not: fly—land—crouch—repair—repair—repair—fly.

### Fork in the Orun South Line (16 years old)

That early winter, Anna and Hagen flew an emergency transport mission.

In a small settlement called Stone Bridge in the Orun Plateau canyon—three hundred people—the fish-salting season has arrived, but they're short of salt. Salt is transported from the north, the fastest route cutting directly across the northern edge of the Orun Plateau. The salt shortage isn't a survival crisis—but three thousand fish are waiting on racks for salt, and in three more days they'll have to be thrown into the river. A crucial source of protein for the entire settlement year. Hagen takes the job. Anna drives.

As we climbed past the central plains and up to the northern edge of Orun, problems arose. The predicted disturbance was much wider than it actually was—a dark gray wall of clouds stretched across the flight path, its base churning with tearing textures of shear airflow.

"The air pressure is dropping," Anna said. "Faster than predicted."

Hagen said nothing. His hands were on his knees. He was waiting for her to make a decision—about the teaching method for the last six months.

Two options. Go through—she can manage, the shortest total flight time, and the fastest way to get the salt. Take the 60-kilometer detour—much safer, but an extra hour and a half.

An hour and a half. Three thousand fish are waiting at Stone Bridge.

Anna's hand gripped the steering wheel. She would get through. She could get through. She wasn't a novice.

Then she felt it—the tightness in her chest.

It wasn't fear. It wasn't excitement. It was a push more familiar than either. "Someone's waiting, I can't keep them waiting any longer." "The Freedmans are reliable." "What will Hagen think if I take the detour?"—even though she knew Hagen would never look down on her for choosing the safe route.

These thoughts didn't come in a queue. They were all mixed together, pushing from the belly all the way to the fingertips. The force said: Go through it. You can do it.

Anna recognized the shape of the thrust.

Not entirely clear—she was sixteen, not forty. But she recognized its shape. She'd seen it that day in the workshop—the thing behind the force that sent the wrench flying from her hand.

She wasn't sure if it was the same one this time. Maybe wearing it before really was the right thing to do. She couldn't quite figure it out in this moment—her hands on the steering wheel, the cloud wall looming ahead.

But she can do one thing: when things are uncertain, she leaves room for flexibility.

"bypass."

Hagen nodded slightly. His chin moved only two millimeters.

The journey outside wasn't entirely calm—the marginal airflow was angular, and the airship glided through the crosswinds like a person walking on gravel—but there was no danger. We arrived at Stone Bridge an hour and ten minutes late. The salt had been unloaded. The fish were saved.

That night, Anna sat on the deck with her feet dangling over the side. The dark silhouette of the Orun Plateau resembled a sleeping behemoth in the starlight.

She didn't know if her decision was right. What if she had made it through? Maybe nothing would have happened. Maybe she would have saved an hour and a half. Maybe someone would have avoided the wait. This was another thing she was learning: most choices don't offer a "right answer." If you choose the safe route, you never know what the other path will hold. You can only keep flying with uncertainty.

Hagen was also on deck that night. The two remained silent for a long time. Then he spoke—not as a comment, but to tell his own story.

"When I was twenty-five, I worked at Orun's South Edge Express. Someone was urgently waiting. There was a wall of clouds in front of me. I passed through it."

Anna looked at him.

"Did they go through it?"

"It made it through. It's okay." His expression was obscured by the starlight. "But that night I sat there until dawn, my hands shaking the whole time. Not because of the bumps. It was because when I made that decision, I wasn't thinking about wind speed or structural stress. I was thinking about how the recipient would react if they got impatient."

He poured the cold tea into the darkness below the deck.

"You went around in circles today. Maybe you went the wrong way. Maybe you should wear it next time. The key isn't whether you went around in circles or wore it—the key is that when you made the decision, you knew whether the hand pushing you was the wind or yourself. You knew that today. That's more valuable than arriving an hour and a half early."

Anna sat on the deck for a while longer. The lights of Stone Bridge looked like a handful of fireflies scattered in the dark valley.

She remembered Miriam's words from three years ago—"You've been pretending you're okay for six months." Those words, spoken when she was thirteen, were a heavy blow. Now she began to understand: recognizing one's own state wasn't a one-time realization, but a practice, and the kind of practice that could never be perfected in a lifetime.

[Design Note] "Is the hand that pushes you the wind or yourself?"—Hagen is unaware that he's repeating something Anna has already been doing (three-second pause). But he gives it a flying metaphor, imbuing Anna's traumatic product with professional dignity. The weight this line carries is different when it reappears in the present-time race in Ep25—it has been used and tested for six years by then.

### Rafale

Anna is seventeen years old. She discovers a Class II airship that has been declared scrapped in an abandoned berth in Frontier City.

The airbags were leaking, the transmission was rusted, three control cables were broken, and there were more patches on the skin than on the original material. The last pilot who managed it parked it here and never returned; the airport declared it scrap and ordered it to be dismantled.

Anna crouched down and tapped the keel. The sound was deep and steady, without any hollowness. She touched the main structural beam—nano-reinforced concrete from the old era, rusted on the surface but intact inside.

"The skeleton is fine," she told Hagen. "I can fix everything else."

"You're crazy."

She convinced Guild to grant her permission to repair and reintegrate the decommissioned ship into the Loom fleet by offering three months of free repair work—maintenance of public facilities at Frontier Airport. It then took four months to repair. It wasn't done by one person—Joren sent parts from Atheria, Hagen's crew helped on their days off, and several people Anna had previously helped in other settlements also came to lend a hand. The day it was finished, Guild officially assigned it to her for management and operation.

She named it "Gust." During its first test flight, a sudden gust of wind blew a loosely secured toolbox off the deck, sending it crashing into a sweet potato stall tent below. The stall owner poked his head out of the tent and shouted into the sky, "That gust of wind almost killed me!"

Anna lost the money for two sweet potatoes.

During the four months of ship repairs, Anna heard the port's steam whistles and organ almost every day.
